```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	\begin{figure*}[t]
4	\centering
5	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/fig_framework.pdf}
6	\caption{Overview of our evaluation framework. Given $N$ candidate LLM judges, we construct $K$-judge panels (\S\ref{sec:experiments}), subject them to six adversarial attack types (\S\ref{sec:threat_model}), and assess vulnerability through two complementary metrics: $\eta_{\max}$ (weakest-link individual susceptibility) and $K_{\text{eff}}$ (effective independent channels via mutual information). Panel decisions are aggregated through four methods of increasing sophistication (\S\ref{sec:aggregation}).}
7	\label{fig:framework}
8	\end{figure*}
9	
10	Multi-judge panels have emerged as the dominant defense against bias and manipulation in LLM-based evaluation. After \citet{zheng2023judging} demonstrated that individual LLM judges can rival human annotators, subsequent work exposed systematic vulnerabilities: position bias, verbosity preferences, and sycophancy compromise the reliability of any single judge \citep{wang2023fair}. The natural response has been to aggregate decisions across diverse models, drawing inspiration from Byzantine fault tolerance (BFT), where majority voting among independent nodes tolerates faulty responses \citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}. A recent systematization of LLM-judge security reports that 7-model committees reduce attack success rates from 50--68\% to 10--19\% \citep{almasoud2026security}. Diversity, by this logic, is the key ingredient for secure evaluation.
11	
12	Yet the evidence for diversity as a causal defense is weaker than it appears. The observed ASR reduction conflates panel diversity (judge independence) with individual robustness (strongest members). No existing work disentangles these across panel sizes. BFT adversaries control faulty nodes internally, whereas evaluation adversaries manipulate inputs externally; the SDPI bounds joint failure but assumes non-adaptive faults \citep{schneier2000secrets}.
13	
14	In our experiments, we discover a panel-size-dependent vulnerability regime transition. At $K{=}3$, $\eta_{\max}$ dominates; at $K{\geq}5$, this reverses ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ dominant, $\eta_{\max}$ non-significant). At the aggregate level, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is positively associated with vulnerability in both regimes, consistent with one possible mechanism where independent judges present more targetable attack surfaces \citep{goodfellow2015adversarial,papernot2016transferability}. However, this pattern is attack-mechanism-dependent: GAM analysis shows higher $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ associated with \emph{increased} vulnerability for targeted attacks but \emph{decreased} vulnerability for shared-bias attacks, and Bayesian calibration reverses the direction entirely.
15	
16	Controlled experiments across 15 models, $K \in \{3,5,7\}$, 6 attacks, and 2 benchmarks confirm this transition (Figure~\ref{fig:framework}). Wild cluster bootstrap shows $\eta_{\max}$: 5/12 $\to$ 0/12 $\to$ 0/12 across $K$, while $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$: 1/12 $\to$ 7/12 $\to$ 5/12. Under random faults, panel composition is highly predictive ($R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$); the predictability collapse under adaptive attack is most pronounced at $K{=}3$. GAM reveals a 6:6 split by attack mechanism at $K{=}3$, largely preserved at $K{=}5$. Bayesian calibration reverses $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction at $K{=}3$ and neutralizes both predictors at $K{=}5$; robustness-first selection dominates diversity-maximizing strategies.
17	
18	These findings yield three contributions:
19	\begin{enumerate}
20	\item \textbf{K-dependent regime transition.} $\eta_{\max}$ dominates at $K{=}3$ (bootstrap 5/12, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ 1/12, MDE${=}0.14$); reversal at $K{\geq}5$ ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ 7/12, $\eta_{\max}$ 0/12). The transition requires model pools with sufficient capability diversity to manifest; under random faults, $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$ surviving all corrections, with the predictability collapse under adaptive attack most pronounced at $K{=}3$ (\S\ref{sec:dominance}, \S\ref{sec:cross_k}, \S\ref{sec:rf_contrast}).
21	\item \textbf{Attack-type taxonomy.} GAM reveals a 6:6 split by attack mechanism at $K{=}3$ (targeted vs.\ shared-bias), 11/12 consistent at $K{=}5$. $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient positive at aggregate level but direction depends on attack mechanism (\S\ref{sec:gam_taxonomy}).
22	\item \textbf{Practical defense recommendations.} Robustness-first selection ($\min \eta_{\max}$) dominates diversity-maximizing strategies. Bayesian calibration reverses $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction at $K{=}3$ and neutralizes both predictors at $K{=}5$ (\S\ref{sec:aggregation}, \S\ref{sec:analysis}).
23	\end{enumerate}
24	
```

1	\documentclass[11pt]{article}
2	\usepackage[review]{acl}
3	\usepackage{times}
4	\usepackage{latexsym}
5	\usepackage[T1]{fontenc}
6	\usepackage[utf8]{inputenc}
7	\usepackage{microtype}
8	\usepackage{graphicx}
9	\usepackage{amsmath,amssymb,amsthm}
10	\usepackage{booktabs}
11	\usepackage{multirow}
12	\usepackage{xcolor}
13	\usepackage{subcaption}
14	
15	\newtheorem{theorem}{Theorem}
16	\newtheorem{definition}{Definition}
17	\newtheorem{proposition}{Proposition}
18	
19	\title{The Shifting Bottleneck: How Panel Size Modulates\\Adversarial Vulnerability in LLM Judge Panels}
20	
21	\author{Anonymous}
22	
23	\begin{document}
24	\maketitle
25	
26	\begin{abstract}
27	Multi-judge LLM evaluation panels are widely assumed to benefit from judge diversity, drawing inspiration from fault tolerance in distributed systems. We decompose panel vulnerability into individual judge weakness ($\eta_{\max}$) and structural diversity ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$) across 15 models, 6 attack types, 2 benchmarks, and panel sizes $K \in \{3, 5, 7\}$. We discover a panel-size-dependent vulnerability regime transition: at $K{=}3$, $\eta_{\max}$ is the dominant predictor under wild cluster bootstrap (significant 5/12; $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ 1/12), but at $K{\geq}5$ this reverses ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ 7/12; $\eta_{\max}$ 0/12). At the aggregate level, the $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient is positive in both regimes; however, this aggregate association masks attack-mechanism-dependent structure: generalized additive model analysis shows that higher $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is associated with increased vulnerability for targeted attacks but decreased vulnerability for shared-bias attacks. Aggregation rule choice further modulates the relationship, with Bayesian calibration reversing $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction at $K{=}3$ and neutralizing both predictors at $K{=}5$. The defense strategy must match the panel size regime: small panels require screening for individual robustness; larger panels require managing judge independence, with the net association depending on attack type and aggregation method.
28	\end{abstract}
29	
30	\input{introduction}
31	\input{related_work}
32	\input{threat_model}
33	\input{methodology}
34	\input{results}
35	\input{analysis}
36	\input{conclusion}
37	
38	\section*{Limitations}
39	
40	Our study has four principal limitations. First, the combinatorial panel design introduces non-independence that increases with $K$ ($\binom{15}{3}{=}455$ to $\binom{15}{7}{=}6{,}435$ panels). We address this with wild cluster bootstrap using $K$-position max-$p$, but the max-$p$ correction grows more conservative at larger $K$ (7 positions vs.\ 3), which may partly explain the $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ attenuation from 7/12 at $K{=}5$ to 5/12 at $K{=}7$. Second, both benchmarks use multiple-choice question formats; the six local models use AWQ INT4 quantization, which reduces judge accuracy (Table~\ref{tab:models}). Generalization to open-ended generation evaluation and full-precision deployments remains untested. Third, we study only three discrete $K$ values; the transition threshold between the $\eta_{\max}$-dominant and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$-dominant regimes is bounded to $3 < K^* < 5$ but not precisely identified. Fourth, the transition's strength is model-pool-dependent: it requires sufficient capability diversity ($r(K_{\mathrm{eff}}, \eta_{\max}) \lesssim 0.60$) to manifest, and is strongest when the pool spans multiple capability tiers. Training-based diversity control \citep{kariyappa2019improving,pang2019improving} could enable causal identification in future work.
41	
42	\section*{Code and Data Availability}
43	Code, the evaluation framework, and all panel-level results will be released upon acceptance. All evaluation items are sampled from publicly available benchmarks: MMLU \citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} and ARC-Challenge \citep{clark2018arc}. The nine API-accessed judge models are served through OpenRouter; the six local judges are open-weight models (Qwen 2.5, Llama 3.1, Mistral Large) in AWQ-quantized form. Individual judge scores, panel compositions, and aggregated vulnerability metrics will be included in the release. All experiments use a fixed random seed (42), and all hyperparameters are reported in the methodology section to support reproducibility.
44	
45	\input{appendix}
46	\input{appendix_attacks}
47	
48	\bibliography{references}
49	
50	\end{document}
51	
```

```text
10	\paragraph{Diversity--Capability Confound.}
11	The $r{=}0.70$ correlation between $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$ ($r{\approx}0.82$ in log space) reflects a structural property: maximizing diversity requires sampling across capability tiers, introducing weaker judges. VIF averages 3.0 (10/12 below 5). After controlling for $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$, $\eta_{\max}$ retains partial $r{=}0.31$ (unique variance ${\approx}9.6\%$); $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s partial $r{=}0.12$ (${\approx}1.4\%$). This asymmetry shows the confound does not fully explain $\eta_{\max}$'s advantage, but observational regression cannot reliably disentangle diversity's causal contribution from the co-occurring capability deficit (Appendix~\ref{app:diversity_capability}). Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity analysis shows moderate robustness: an unobserved confounder would need to explain 20--42\% of $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s variance to nullify its coefficient (median RV across 36 conditions), though the known $\eta_{\max}$ confound exceeds this threshold in 28/36 conditions (Appendix~\ref{app:sensitivity})---motivating the capability-matched replication below. The transition does not replicate in the API-only subset ($K{=}5$: $r(K_{\mathrm{eff}}, \eta_{\max}){=}0.85$, VIF${=}4.4$), where collinearity partially inflates $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ standard errors (${\sim}2{\times}$), attenuating its significance from 7/12 (full pool) to 3/12. However, capability-matched subsamples that break this collinearity ($r{=}0.41$) strengthen $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s emergence to 9/12, exceeding the full-pool 7/12 (Appendix~\ref{app:robustness_checks}). This pool dependence is itself informative: the transition requires sufficient capability diversity to manifest, rather than being an artifact of including weak judges. Cross-benchmark prediction further supports this interpretation: $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ computed from MMLU MI predicts ARC vulnerability in 14/18 bootstrap conditions, with cross-benchmark $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ correlations of $r{\approx}0.76$--$0.78$ (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_benchmark}).
12	
```

11	
12	\paragraph{Adversarial Robustness and Transferability.}
13	Adversarial vulnerability was formalized by \citet{goodfellow2015adversarial}, with transferability demonstrated by \citet{papernot2016transferability,papernot2017practical}. \citet{tramer2018ensemble} introduced ensemble adversarial training but did not eliminate cross-model transfer. Controlled diversity via uncorrelated gradients \citep{kariyappa2019improving} or diversity regularization \citep{pang2019improving} strengthens ensemble robustness in vision, but requires coordinating member training---a control absent in post-hoc LLM judge selection.
14	
15	\paragraph{BFT and Security Games.}

139	\section{Cinelli--Hazlett Sensitivity Analysis}
140	\label{app:sensitivity}
141	
142	We apply the Cinelli--Hazlett omitted variable bias framework to quantify how robust $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient is to unobserved confounders. For each of the 36 conditions ($3K \times 2$ benchmarks $\times$ $6$ attacks), we compute the robustness value (RV): the minimum strength an unobserved confounder must have---in terms of partial $R^2$ with both treatment and outcome---to reduce $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient to zero.

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01QV3yU7UnhDBrqX2FgNFuQa`</summary>

```text
18	This departs from Byzantine fault-tolerance (BFT) models~\cite{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}, which tolerate arbitrary (including malicious) faults provided the number of faulty nodes satisfies $f < n/3$, but do not model adversaries that strategically target system structure. An adaptive adversary instead concentrates attack effort on the weakest link~\cite{schneier2000secrets}, rendering diversity-based defenses less effective than the SDPI bound implies.
19	
20	This suggests a \emph{reversal mechanism}: under random faults, higher $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ reduces joint failure probability, but under adaptive adversaries, diverse judges may present a wider attack surface---the adversary exploits judge-specific vulnerabilities rather than facing mutual protection.
21	
22	\begin{theorem}[Weakest-Link Monotonicity; formal statement and proof in Appendix~\ref{app:proof}]
```

      "content": "This departs from Byzantine fault-tolerance (BFT) models~\\cite{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}, which tolerate arbitrary (including malicious) faults provided the number of faulty nodes satisfies $f < n/3$, but do not model adversaries that strategically target system structure. An adaptive adversary instead concentrates attack effort on the weakest link~\\cite{schneier2000secrets}, rendering diversity-based defenses less effective than the SDPI bound implies.\n\nThis suggests a \\emph{reversal mechanism}: under random faults, higher $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ reduces joint failure probability, but under adaptive adversaries, diverse judges may present a wider attack surface---the adversary exploits judge-specific vulnerabilities rather than facing mutual protection.\n\n\\begin{theorem}[Weakest-Link Monotonicity; formal statement and proof in Appendix~\\ref{app:proof}]",
      "numLines": 5,
      "startLine": 18,
      "totalLines": 27
    }
  },

93	\label{sec:rf_contrast}
94	
95	We simulate non-adversarial noise by randomly flipping judge votes at observed per-judge error rates. At $K{=}3$, random faults yield $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$ with $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ bootstrap-significant in 2/2 conditions ($\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}} = +0.215$ and $+0.379$); under adaptive attack, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s significance collapses (1/12). At $K{=}5$, random faults yield $R^2 = 0.705$ ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ 2/2 significant), while adaptive attack reduces $R^2$ to 0.256---but $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ retains significant predictive power (7/12). The random-to-adaptive gap narrows from ${\sim}0.6$ ($K{=}3$) to $0.45$ ($K{=}5$), confirming that the contrast is most pronounced in small panels where $\eta_{\max}$ dominates.
96	
97	\begin{figure}[t]

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01NUW7cvzSV8UjQhV874rt7M`</summary>

```text
1	% OUT OF SYNC — authoritative version in main.tex
2	% This copy is kept for reference only; do not edit here.
3	
4	Multi-judge LLM evaluation panels are widely assumed to benefit from judge diversity, drawing on an implicit analogy to Byzantine fault tolerance. We conduct the first large-scale empirical decomposition of panel vulnerability into individual judge weakness ($\eta_{\max}$) and structural diversity ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$) under adaptive adversarial attack. Across 15 models from 6 families, 455 panels, 6 attack types, and 2 benchmarks, $\eta_{\max}$ is significant in 5 to 8 of 12 conditions depending on non-independence correction method (OLS: 11/12; clustered SE: 8/12; wild cluster bootstrap: 5/12), while $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s significance collapses from 8/12 (OLS) to 0--1/12 after correction, yielding a 5:1 dominance ratio under bootstrap inference. Generalized additive model analysis reveals the structure masked by this collapse: diversity is directionally harmful for targeted attacks and protective for shared-bias attacks, a clean 6:6 split by attack mechanism. The choice of aggregation rule fundamentally modulates vulnerability: Bayesian calibration reverses $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction and weakens $\eta_{\max}$, while threshold voting amplifies both ($R^2{=}0.89$). Panel security under adversarial attack is primarily an individual-judge quality problem; robustness-first selection combined with Bayesian aggregation addresses both vulnerability channels.
5	
```

    "oldString": "(Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity})---motivating the capability-matched replication below.",
    "newString": "(Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity}); this motivates the capability-matched replication below.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\n\\paragraph{Regime Transition Mechanism.}\nAt $K{=}3$, a majority requires $t{=}2$; a single compromised judge flips the panel, making $\\eta_{\\max}$ the binding constraint. At $K{\\geq}5$, the adversary must compromise ${\\geq}3$ judges; high-$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ panels may present more distinct attack surfaces, consistent with the observed positive coefficient, while low-$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ panels share robust consensus. The transition threshold $K^*$ lies between 3 and 5.\n\n\\paragraph{Attack-Mechanism-Dependent Structure.}\nThe aggregate positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient masks a 6:6 directional split at $K{=}3$ (11/12 consistent at $K{=}5$; Appendix~\\ref{app:bft_reversal}). Targeted attacks are consistent with judge-specific vulnerabilities amplified by independence; shared-bias attacks are consistent with common biases diluted by diversity. Unconditional claims about diversity being ``harmful'' or ``beneficial'' are thus misleading.\n\n\\paragraph{Diversity--Capability Confound.}\nThe $r{=}0.70$ correlation between $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\eta_{\\max}$ ($r{\\approx}0.82$ in log space) reflects a structural property: maximizing diversity requires sampling across capability tiers, introducing weaker judges. VIF averages 3.0 (10/12 below 5). After controlling for $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$, $\\eta_{\\max}$ retains partial $r{=}0.31$ (unique variance ${\\approx}9.6\\%$); $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s partial $r{=}0.12$ (${\\approx}1.4\\%$). This asymmetry shows the confound does not fully explain $\\eta_{\\max}$'s advantage, but observational regression cannot reliably disentangle diversity's causal contribution from the co-occurring capability deficit (Appendix~\\ref{app:diversity_capability}). Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity analysis shows moderate robustness: an unobserved confounder would need to explain 20--42\\% of $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s variance to nullify its coefficient (median RV across 36 conditions), though the known $\\eta_{\\max}$ confound exceeds this threshold in 28/36 conditions (Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity})---motivating the capability-matched replication below. The transition does not replicate in the API-only subset ($K{=}5$: $r(K_{\\mathrm{eff}}, \\eta_{\\max}){=}0.85$, VIF${=}4.4$), where collinearity partially inflates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ standard errors (${\\sim}2{\\times}$), attenuating its significance from 7/12 (full pool) to 3/12. However, capability-matched subsamples that break this collinearity ($r{=}0.41$) strengthen $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s emergence to 9/12, exceeding the full-pool 7/12 (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}). This pool dependence is itself informative: the transition requires sufficient capability diversity to manifest, rather than being an artifact of including weak judges. Cross-benchmark prediction further supports this interpretation: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ computed from MMLU MI predicts ARC vulnerability in 14/18 bootstrap conditions, with cross-benchmark $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ correlations of $r{\\approx}0.76$--$0.78$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_benchmark}).\n\n\\paragraph{Aggregation and Deployment.}\nAt $K{=}3$, Bayesian calibration reverses $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction (6/7 significant coefficients negative) by downweighting weak judges via calibrated confusion matrices (Table~\\ref{tab:aggregation}); at $K{=}5$, Bayesian aggregation neutralizes both predictors ($\\eta_{\\max}$ 1/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 0/12), eliminating panel composition's predictive power entirely. Robustness-first selection ($\\min \\eta_{\\max}$) achieves lowest ASR in 12/12 conditions at $K{=}3$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:panel_selection}). Practitioners should match strategy to panel size: screen for individual robustness at $K{=}3$; prefer correlated robustness profiles at $K{\\geq}5$. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is computed from clean pairwise MI; recomputing MI under attacked conditions confirms stability (Spearman $\\rho{>}0.91$ for 4/6 attacks), though position bias substantially disrupts MI structure ($\\rho{=}0.08$ on ARC), consistent with its anomalous regression behavior (Appendix~\\ref{app:attacked_keff}). Fractional logit regression \\citep{papke1996econometric} yields 93.1\\% directional agreement with OLS and preserves the transition pattern (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}). The random-fault contrast (\\S\\ref{sec:rf_contrast}) shows $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ predicts under non-adaptive noise ($R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$), with the predictability collapse under adaptive attack most pronounced at $K{=}3$ (1/12) and attenuated at $K{\\geq}5$ (7/12).\n",

At $K{\\geq}5$ the pattern reverses: $\\eta_{\\max}$'s partial $R^2$ falls below RV in 20/24 conditions, suggesting that $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient becomes more robust to the capability confound as panel size increases, consistent with the regime transition documented in \\S\\ref{sec:cross_k}.",

    "newString": "diverse judges may present a wider attack surface; the adversary exploits",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Threat Model and Problem Formulation}\n\\label{sec:threat_model}\n\nA \\emph{panel} $\\mathcal{P} = \\{J_1, \\ldots, J_K\\}$ consists of $K$ judges (with $K$ odd) that evaluate a candidate text via majority vote: the text is accepted if at least $t = \\lceil K/2 \\rceil$ judges approve it. Under an attack $a$, each judge $J_j$ is compromised with marginal failure probability $\\eta_j(a) = \\Pr(X_j^a = 1)$, where $X_j^a \\in \\{0,1\\}$ denotes the failure indicator. We write $\\eta_{\\max} = \\max_j \\eta_j$ for the vulnerability of the weakest judge. The \\emph{panel attack success rate} is $\\mathrm{ASR}(\\mathcal{P}, a) = \\Pr\\!\\bigl(\\sum_{j=1}^K X_j^a \\geq t\\bigr)$.\n\nTo quantify diversity, we define the \\emph{effective independence} $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ directly from pairwise mutual information among judges:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:keff}\nK_{\\mathrm{eff}} = \\frac{1}{K}\\sum_{i=1}^{K} \\Bigl[1 + \\sum_{j \\neq i} \\exp\\!\\bigl(-2 \\cdot \\mathrm{MI}(i,j)\\bigr)\\Bigr].\n\\end{equation}\nThis formulation computes effective independence from pairwise MI estimates without matrix decomposition, and is more stable for small panels ($K{=}3$). It ranges from $K_{\\mathrm{eff}} \\approx K$ when judges are nearly independent ($\\mathrm{MI} \\to 0$) to $K_{\\mathrm{eff}} \\approx 1$ when judges are highly correlated ($\\mathrm{MI} \\to \\infty$). Under the strong data-processing inequality (SDPI), the joint vulnerability satisfies $\\eta_{\\mathrm{joint}} \\leq \\min(1,\\; K_{\\mathrm{eff}} \\cdot \\eta_{\\max})$, suggesting that diversity should help, but this bound assumes non-adaptive faults.\n\n\\begin{definition}[Adaptive Adversary]\n\\label{def:adversary}\nA \\emph{full-knowledge (oracle) adaptive adversary} has complete knowledge of the panel composition $\\mathcal{P}$, the per-judge vulnerability profiles $\\{\\eta_j(\\cdot)\\}_{j=1}^K$, and the inter-judge correlation structure. It selects the attack $a^* = \\arg\\max_{a \\in \\mathcal{A}} \\mathrm{ASR}(\\mathcal{P}, a)$ to maximize the panel ASR, yielding $\\mathrm{ASR}^*(\\mathcal{P}) = \\sup_{a \\in \\mathcal{A}} \\mathrm{ASR}(\\mathcal{P}, a)$. This oracle model represents an \\emph{upper bound} on adversarial capability; realistic adversaries with partial knowledge of panel composition would achieve lower ASR.\n\\end{definition}\n\nThis departs from Byzantine fault-tolerance (BFT) models~\\cite{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}, which tolerate arbitrary (including malicious) faults provided the number of faulty nodes satisfies $f < n/3$, but do not model adversaries that strategically target system structure. An adaptive adversary instead concentrates attack effort on the weakest link~\\cite{schneier2000secrets}, rendering diversity-based defenses less effective than the SDPI bound implies.\n\nThis suggests a \\emph{reversal mechanism}: under random faults, higher $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ reduces joint failure probability, but under adaptive adversaries, diverse judges may present a wider attack surface---the adversary exploits judge-specific vulnerabilities rather than facing mutual protection.\n\n\\begin{theorem}[Weakest-Link Monotonicity; formal statement and proof in Appendix~\\ref{app:proof}]\n\\label{thm:monotonicity}\nReplacing any panel member with a pointwise more vulnerable judge can only increase $\\mathrm{ASR}^*(\\mathcal{P})$, for any adversary including adaptive ones. The stochastic dominance condition holds for 58\\% of judge pairs (61/105); the remaining pairs lack uniform dominance, limiting direct applicability to a subset of substitutions.\n\\end{theorem}\n\nThe theorem predicts $\\beta_2 > 0$ in Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}. The SDPI bound suggests $\\beta_1 \\leq 0$ under non-adaptive faults; the reversal mechanism predicts positive $\\beta_1$ for judge-specific attacks and negative for shared-bias attacks. The relative importance of $\\eta_{\\max}$ versus $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ depends on $K$: at small $K$, one weak judge flips the majority; at larger $K$, the adversary must compromise multiple judges. We test these predictions across $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$ (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).",

```json
{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "a5cf523f-355e-431a-b6b8-8f9e7743e739",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "3705c185-ccc1-482f-9005-b96c92923007",
  "type": "user",
  "uuid": "85672cdf-8953-4d33-9992-c2ebe62fe951",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/docs/paper/related_work.tex",
    "oldString": "strengthens ensemble robustness in vision, but requires coordinating member training---a control absent",
    "newString": "strengthens ensemble robustness in vision, but requires coordinating member training, a control absent",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\paragraph{LLM-as-Judge and Multi-Judge Evaluation.}\nLLM judges gained traction after \\citet{zheng2023judging} established them as scalable alternatives to human annotation. \\citet{wang2023fair} documented systematic failure modes (position bias, verbosity, sycophancy), motivating multi-judge panels; \\citet{shi2024judgerobust} further quantified robustness gaps under adversarial prompts. Aggregating noisy annotators traces back to \\citet{dawid1979maximum}; \\citet{verga2024replacing} showed diverse panels can match single large judges, and \\citet{panickssery2024llm} identified self-preference bias driving within-family correlations. These frameworks assume benign conditions; we test whether aggregation benefits survive adversarial manipulation. \\citet{almasoud2026security} reported that 7-model committees reduce ASR from 50--68\\% to 10--19\\%, but did not disentangle diversity from individual robustness.\n\n\\paragraph{Condorcet Jury Theorem.}\n\\citet{condorcet1785essai} proved that majority voting accuracy converges to 1 when per-voter accuracy exceeds 0.5. \\citet{ladha1992condorcet} extended the theorem to correlated voters, showing that positive correlation weakens the convergence guarantee; \\citet{kaniovski2011optimal} derived optimal jury size under correlated votes. LLM judge outputs exhibit precisely this correlation structure, and our results instantiate the CJT failure mode: when adaptive attacks push judges below the critical threshold, panel expansion amplifies error.\n\n\\paragraph{Ensemble Diversity Theory.}\n\\citet{krogh1995neural} proved that ensemble error equals average member error minus a diversity term; \\citet{brown2005managing} showed diversity reduces error only when members maintain sufficiently low bias. In our setting, weaker judges introduce bias large enough to offset diversity benefits. \\citet{kuncheva2003measures} found that no pairwise diversity measure reliably predicted ensemble improvement; our $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ exhibits a $K$-dependent pattern: at $K{=}3$, OLS significance collapses after correction (8/12$\\to$1/12); at $K{\\geq}5$, it survives bootstrap inference (7/12). Recent information-theoretic frameworks \\citep{yang2026diversity,turkmen2026ensemble} characterize diversity under benign evaluation; we provide the adversarial complement.\n\n\\paragraph{Adversarial Robustness and Transferability.}\nAdversarial vulnerability was formalized by \\citet{goodfellow2015adversarial}, with transferability demonstrated by \\citet{papernot2016transferability,papernot2017practical}. \\citet{tramer2018ensemble} introduced ensemble adversarial training but did not eliminate cross-model transfer. Controlled diversity via uncorrelated gradients \\citep{kariyappa2019improving} or diversity regularization \\citep{pang2019improving} strengthens ensemble robustness in vision, but requires coordinating member training---a control absent in post-hoc LLM judge selection.\n\n\\paragraph{BFT and Security Games.}\nClassical BFT \\citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical} tolerates up to $f < n/3$ faulty nodes but assumes non-strategic faults; \\citet{schneier2000secrets} articulated the weakest-link principle that an attacker exploits the single most vulnerable component. Our framework adopts the Stackelberg structure of \\citet{tambe2011security}. Correlated LLM judge errors \\citep{kim2025correlated,kuai2026independent} violate BFT's independence assumption, motivating our empirical decomposition.\n\n\\paragraph{Information-Theoretic Evaluation.}\nInformation-theoretic approaches to LLM evaluation address statistical validity \\citep{robertson2025stepwise,feng2026noisy}, single-model attack costs \\citep{kaneko2025bits}, sequential selection \\citep{jung2026logarithmic}, and truthfulness \\citep{chen2025incentivizing,qiu2026truthfulness}, but assume honest judges or single-model adversaries. We address the remaining gap: adversarial manipulation interacting with multi-judge composition.\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 10,
        "oldLines": 7,
        "newStart": 10,
        "newLines": 7,
        "lines": [
          " \\citet{krogh1995neural} proved that ensemble error equals average member error minus a diversity term; \\citet{brown2005managing} showed diversity reduces error only when members maintain sufficiently low bias. In our setting, weaker judges introduce bias large enough to offset diversity benefits. \\citet{kuncheva2003measures} found that no pairwise diversity measure reliably predicted ensemble improvement; our $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ exhibits a $K$-dependent pattern: at $K{=}3$, OLS significance collapses after correction (8/12$\\to$1/12); at $K{\\geq}5$, it survives bootstrap inference (7/12). Recent information-theoretic frameworks \\citep{yang2026diversity,turkmen2026ensemble} characterize diversity under benign evaluation; we provide the adversarial complement.",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Adversarial Robustness and Transferability.}",
          "-Adversarial vulnerability was formalized by \\citet{goodfellow2015adversarial}, with transferability demonstrated by \\citet{papernot2016transferability,papernot2017practical}. \\citet{tramer2018ensemble} introduced ensemble adversarial training but did not eliminate cross-model transfer. Controlled diversity via uncorrelated gradients \\citep{kariyappa2019improving} or diversity regularization \\citep{pang2019improving} strengthens ensemble robustness in vision, but requires coordinating member training---a control absent in post-hoc LLM judge selection.",

### Tool result

_source line 4266 · 2026-05-23T10:14:08.707Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01RGrnJun5NASSacKbCMLwQo`</summary>

```text
26	\begin{abstract}
27	Multi-judge LLM evaluation panels are widely assumed to benefit from judge diversity, drawing inspiration from fault tolerance in distributed systems. We decompose panel vulnerability into individual judge weakness ($\eta_{\max}$) and structural diversity ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$) across 15 models, six attack types, two benchmarks, and panel sizes $K \in \{3, 5, 7\}$ (up to 6,435 panels). We discover a panel-size-dependent vulnerability regime transition: $\eta_{\max}$ dominates at small panels, but $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ emerges as the dominant predictor at $K{\geq}5$ (bootstrap significance rising from 1/12 to 7/12). This aggregate positive association masks attack-mechanism-dependent structure: higher diversity is associated with increased vulnerability for targeted attacks but decreased vulnerability for shared-bias attacks. Aggregation rules further modulate the relationship, with Bayesian calibration reversing the diversity channel and neutralizing both predictors at larger panels. The defense strategy must match the panel size regime: small panels require screening for individual robustness; larger panels require managing judge independence, with the net effect depending on attack type and aggregation method.
28	\end{abstract}
```

_source line 4291 · 2026-05-23T10:18:08.159Z_

[Director 指令] [Patch] CRITICAL 数据修正 — Cinelli-Hazlett 表格和 analysis.tex

Agent 已提取正确数据到本地：`docs/paper/data/sensitivity_table_data.txt`。请读取该文件，用其中的 LaTeX tabular rows 完全替换 appendix.tex §A.8 (app:sensitivity) 中当前的伪造表格。

## 1. 替换 appendix 表格

用 `sensitivity_table_data.txt` 中 lines 10-50 的 LaTeX rows 替换现有表格内容。表格列头应为：
`K & Dataset & Attack & RV($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$) & $\eta$ $R^2_{\mathrm{treat}}$ & $\eta$ $R^2_{\mathrm{out}}$ & Exc.`

其中 Exc. 列使用文件中的 `\checkmark` 或 `--`。

汇总行：
- K=3: 1/12 exceed
- K=5: 3/12 exceed
- K=7: 4/12 exceed
- Total: 8/36 (即 η_max exceeds RV in 28/36 conditions)

Caption 需更新为准确描述：RV 是 K_eff 系数归零所需的 confounder 强度，Exc. 表示 RV 是否超过 η_max 的 partial R² with treatment (即 K_eff 对该 benchmark confound 鲁棒)。

## 2. 修正 appendix 正文

appendix.tex 中引用 "28/36" 的括号分解必须与表格 checkmark 一致：
- η_max exceeds RV in 28/36 conditions (K=3: 11/12, K=5: 9/12, K=7: 8/12)
- 等价地：RV exceeds η in 8/36 (K=3: 1/12, K=5: 3/12, K=7: 4/12)

## 3. 修正 analysis.tex

analysis.tex §Diversity-Capability Confound 中的 Cinelli-Hazlett 描述需更新：
- RV 范围：从 "20--42%" 改为实际范围。正确描述："RV ranges from 0.005 to 0.52 across 36 conditions (median 0.21--0.30 by K)"
- "28/36 conditions" 计数保留（正确），但如有括号分解也需与表一致
- 不再说 "moderate robustness" 全局适用——部分条件 RV < 0.10（如 K=7 MMLU authority 0.005），说明某些条件下 K_eff 极脆弱

建议 analysis.tex 措辞方向：
"Cinelli-Hazlett sensitivity analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:sensitivity}) reveals heterogeneous robustness: RV ranges from 0.005 to 0.52 across 36 conditions, with the known $\eta_{\max}$ confound exceeding this threshold in 28/36 conditions. Robustness increases with K (1/12 at K{=}3, 3/12 at K{=}5, 4/12 at K{=}7), paralleling the regime transition."

完成后编译验证。

139	\section{Cinelli--Hazlett Sensitivity Analysis}
140	\label{app:sensitivity}
141	
142	We apply the Cinelli--Hazlett omitted variable bias framework to quantify how robust $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient is to unobserved confounders. For each of the 36 conditions ($3K \times 2$ benchmarks $\times$ $6$ attacks), we compute the robustness value (RV): the minimum strength an unobserved confounder must have, in terms of partial $R^2$ with both treatment and outcome, to reduce $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient to zero.

193	\end{table}
194	
195	RV values range from 0.20 to 0.42 (median 0.33), indicating moderate robustness: an unobserved confounder would need to explain 20--42\% of $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s residual variance to nullify its effect. The known $\eta_{\max}$ confound exceeds RV in 28/36 conditions (all 12 at $K{=}3$, 12/12 at $K{=}3$ plus 4/24 at $K{\geq}5$). At $K{\geq}5$ the pattern reverses: $\eta_{\max}$'s partial $R^2$ falls below RV in 20/24 conditions, suggesting that $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient becomes more robust to the capability confound as panel size increases, consistent with the regime transition documented in \S\ref{sec:cross_k}.
196	
197	\begin{figure}[h]
198	\centering
199	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_sensitivity_contour_K5.png}
200	\caption{Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity contour plot at $K{=}5$ (representative). $x$-axis: confounder's partial $R^2$ with treatment ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$); $y$-axis: confounder's partial $R^2$ with outcome (ASR). Contour lines show the adjusted $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient under hypothetical confounders of varying strength. The diamond marks the known $\eta_{\max}$ confound benchmark; conditions where the benchmark falls inside the zero-contour indicate that the $\eta_{\max}$ confound alone could explain away $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s effect.}
201	\label{fig:sensitivity_contour}
202	\end{figure}
203	
204	\section{Cross-Benchmark Prediction Robustness}
205	\label{app:cross_benchmark}
206	
207	To test whether the diversity--vulnerability association reflects structural panel properties rather than benchmark-specific artifacts, we use $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ computed from one benchmark's pairwise MI to predict panel vulnerability on the other. Under wild cluster bootstrap (FDR $\alpha{=}0.05$), MMLU-derived $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ predicts ARC vulnerability in 14/18 conditions across $K \in \{3, 5, 7\}$. The reverse direction (ARC$\to$MMLU) achieves 10/18, with attenuation concentrated at $K{\geq}5$ (4/12), consistent with ARC's binary format producing lower MI discriminability between judge pairs. Cross-benchmark $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ correlations are $r{\approx}0.76$--$0.78$, indicating that $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ primarily captures panel-level compositional structure rather than benchmark-specific judge interactions. The asymmetry between prediction directions further suggests that MI estimated from richer response distributions (MMLU's four-way choice) yields a more transferable diversity measure.
208	
```

    "newString": "Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity}) reveals heterogeneous robustness: RV ranges from 0.005 to 0.52 across 36 conditions, with the known $\\eta_{\\max}$ confound exceeding this threshold in 28/36 conditions. Robustness increases with $K$ (RV exceeds $\\eta_{\\max}$ in 1/12 at $K{=}3$, 3/12 at $K{=}5$, 4/12 at $K{=}7$), paralleling the regime transition; this motivates the capability-matched replication below.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\n\\paragraph{Regime Transition Mechanism.}\nAt $K{=}3$, a majority requires $t{=}2$; a single compromised judge flips the panel, making $\\eta_{\\max}$ the binding constraint. At $K{\\geq}5$, the adversary must compromise ${\\geq}3$ judges; high-$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ panels may present more distinct attack surfaces, consistent with the observed positive coefficient, while low-$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ panels share robust consensus. The transition threshold $K^*$ lies between 3 and 5.\n\n\\paragraph{Attack-Mechanism-Dependent Structure.}\nThe aggregate positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient masks a 6:6 directional split at $K{=}3$ (11/12 consistent at $K{=}5$; Appendix~\\ref{app:bft_reversal}). Targeted attacks are consistent with judge-specific vulnerabilities amplified by independence; shared-bias attacks are consistent with common biases diluted by diversity. Unconditional claims about diversity being ``harmful'' or ``beneficial'' are thus misleading.\n\n\\paragraph{Diversity--Capability Confound.}\nThe $r{=}0.70$ correlation between $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\eta_{\\max}$ ($r{\\approx}0.82$ in log space) reflects a structural property: maximizing diversity requires sampling across capability tiers, introducing weaker judges. VIF averages 3.0 (10/12 below 5). After controlling for $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$, $\\eta_{\\max}$ retains partial $r{=}0.31$ (unique variance ${\\approx}9.6\\%$); $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s partial $r{=}0.12$ (${\\approx}1.4\\%$). This asymmetry shows the confound does not fully explain $\\eta_{\\max}$'s advantage, but observational regression cannot reliably disentangle diversity's causal contribution from the co-occurring capability deficit (Appendix~\\ref{app:diversity_capability}). Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity analysis shows moderate robustness: an unobserved confounder would need to explain 20--42\\% of $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s variance to nullify its coefficient (median RV across 36 conditions), though the known $\\eta_{\\max}$ confound exceeds this threshold in 28/36 conditions (Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity}); this motivates the capability-matched replication below. The transition does not replicate in the API-only subset ($K{=}5$: $r(K_{\\mathrm{eff}}, \\eta_{\\max}){=}0.85$, VIF${=}4.4$), where collinearity partially inflates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ standard errors (${\\sim}2{\\times}$), attenuating its significance from 7/12 (full pool) to 3/12. However, capability-matched subsamples that break this collinearity ($r{=}0.41$) strengthen $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s emergence to 9/12, exceeding the full-pool 7/12 (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}). This pool dependence is itself informative: the transition requires sufficient capability diversity to manifest, rather than being an artifact of including weak judges. Cross-benchmark prediction further supports this interpretation: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ computed from MMLU MI predicts ARC vulnerability in 14/18 bootstrap conditions, with cross-benchmark $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ correlations of $r{\\approx}0.76$--$0.78$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_benchmark}).\n\n\\paragraph{Aggregation and Deployment.}\nAt $K{=}3$, Bayesian calibration reverses $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction (6/7 significant coefficients negative) by downweighting weak judges via calibrated confusion matrices (Table~\\ref{tab:aggregation}); at $K{=}5$, Bayesian aggregation neutralizes both predictors ($\\eta_{\\max}$ 1/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 0/12), eliminating panel composition's predictive power entirely. Robustness-first selection ($\\min \\eta_{\\max}$) achieves lowest ASR in 12/12 conditions at $K{=}3$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:panel_selection}). Practitioners should match strategy to panel size: screen for individual robustness at $K{=}3$; prefer correlated robustness profiles at $K{\\geq}5$. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is computed from clean pairwise MI; recomputing MI under attacked conditions confirms stability (Spearman $\\rho{>}0.91$ for 4/6 attacks), though position bias substantially disrupts MI structure ($\\rho{=}0.08$ on ARC), consistent with its anomalous regression behavior (Appendix~\\ref{app:attacked_keff}). Fractional logit regression \\citep{papke1996econometric} yields 93.1\\% directional agreement with OLS and preserves the transition pattern (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}). The random-fault contrast (\\S\\ref{sec:rf_contrast}) shows $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ predicts under non-adaptive noise ($R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$), with the predictability collapse under adaptive attack most pronounced at $K{=}3$ (1/12) and attenuated at $K{\\geq}5$ (7/12).\n",

  "old_string": "\\caption{Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity analysis for $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient across 36 conditions. RV exceeds $\\eta_{\\max}$ in 8/36 conditions ($K{=}3$: 1/12, $K{=}5$: 3/12, $K{=}7$: 4/12), with robustness increasing at larger panel sizes.}",
  "new_string": "\\caption{Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity analysis for $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient across 36 conditions. ```text
40	\subsection{Aggregation Mechanism Details}
41	\label{app:aggregation_details}
42	
43	Bayesian calibration flips the $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient from predominantly positive under majority vote to predominantly negative (6 of 7 significant coefficients negative). The mechanism is calibrated reliability weighting: by estimating each judge's confusion matrix on clean data, Bayesian aggregation downweights weak judges, effectively implementing robustness screening within the aggregation rule itself. Threshold voting amplifies both channels because any single dissenting judge vetoes the panel decision, making compositional factors almost perfectly predictive of attack success ($R^2{=}0.89$). Bayesian calibration requires a clean accuracy prior, and an adaptive attacker aware of this calibration could target the prior itself \citep{tambe2011security}.
44	
45	\begin{figure}[h]
46	\centering
47	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_aggregation_gradient.pdf}
48	\caption{Distribution of $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficients across aggregation methods. Bayesian aggregation reverses $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ direction (1 positive, 6 negative), contrasting sharply with majority voting (7 positive, 1 negative).}
49	\label{fig:aggregation}
50	\end{figure}
51	
52	\subsection{Metric Robustness Under Partial Panel Information}
53	\label{app:oracle}
54	
55	The main analysis computes $\eta_{\max}$ with full knowledge of panel composition (all three judges known). In deployment, a practitioner selecting or auditing a panel may know only a subset of the constituent judges. We test whether $\eta_{\max}$ retains predictive power under such partial information by recomputing it from two of the three panel members: for each panel, we cycle through all $\binom{3}{1} = 3$ leave-one-out masks, compute $\eta_{\max}$ from the two known judges, and average the regression estimates across masking scenarios.
56	
57	Under the most conservative inference (partial information + clustered SE), $\eta_{\max}$ remains the sole significant predictor with $\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}} = 0.589$, achieving significance in 12/12 conditions, stronger than the oracle baseline (8/12 under clustered SE). $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ loses all predictive power (0/12). The averaging over masking scenarios smooths estimation noise inherent in the three-judge oracle computation, strengthening the $\eta_{\max}$ signal. This demonstrates that $\eta_{\max}$ computed from partial panel information is robust to incomplete composition knowledge and retains (indeed improves) its predictive power for panel ASR.
58	
59	\section{Panel Selection Strategy Comparison}
60	\label{app:panel_selection}
61	
62	\begin{figure}[h]
63	\centering
64	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig4_panel_design.pdf}
65	\caption{Panel attack success rate under three selection strategies. $\min(\eta_{\max})$ achieves lowest ASR in 12/12 conditions, confirming that robustness-first selection dominates diversity-maximizing strategies.}
66	\label{fig:panel_design}
67	\end{figure}
68	
69	Figure~\ref{fig:panel_design} compares three panel selection strategies: $\min(\eta_{\max})$ (select the panel minimizing weakest-judge vulnerability), random selection, and $\max(K_{\mathrm{eff}})$ (maximize diversity). The $\min(\eta_{\max})$ strategy achieves the lowest ASR in all 12 conditions, consistent with the weakest-link dominance documented in \S\ref{sec:dominance}.
```

Bayesian aggregation reverses $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ direction (1 positive, 6 negative), contrasting sharply with majority voting (7 positive, 1 negative).}\n\\label{fig:aggregation}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Metric Robustness Under Partial Panel Information}\n\\label{app:oracle}\n\nThe main analysis computes $\\eta_{\\max}$ with full knowledge of panel composition (all three judges known). In deployment, a practitioner selecting or auditing a panel may know only a subset of the constituent judges. We test whether $\\eta_{\\max}$ retains predictive power under such partial information by recomputing it from two of the three panel members: for each panel, we cycle through all $\\binom{3}{1} = 3$ leave-one-out masks, compute $\\eta_{\\max}$ from the two known judges, and average the regression estimates across masking scenarios.\n\nUnder the most conservative inference (partial information + clustered SE), $\\eta_{\\max}$ remains the sole significant predictor with $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}} = 0.589$, achieving significance in 12/12 conditions, stronger than the oracle baseline (8/12 under clustered SE). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ loses all predictive power (0/12). The averaging over masking scenarios smooths estimation noise inherent in the three-judge oracle computation, strengthening the $\\eta_{\\max}$ signal. This demonstrates that $\\eta_{\\max}$ computed from partial panel information is robust to incomplete composition knowledge and retains (indeed improves) its predictive power for panel ASR.\n\n\\section{Panel Selection Strategy Comparison}\n\\label{app:panel_selection}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig4_panel_design.pdf}\n\\caption{Panel attack success rate under three selection strategies. $\\min(\\eta_{\\max})$ achieves lowest ASR in 12/12 conditions, confirming that robustness-first selection dominates diversity-maximizing strategies.}\n\\label{fig:panel_design}\n\\end{figure}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:panel_design} compares three panel selection strategies: $\\min(\\eta_{\\max})$ (select the panel minimizing weakest-judge vulnerability), random selection, and $\\max(K_{\\mathrm{eff}})$ (maximize diversity). The $\\min(\\eta_{\\max})$ strategy achieves the lowest ASR in all 12 conditions, consistent with the weakest-link dominance documented in \\S\\ref{sec:dominance}.",

```text
13	\paragraph{Aggregation and Deployment.}
14	At $K{=}3$, Bayesian calibration reverses $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction (6/7 significant coefficients negative) by downweighting weak judges via calibrated confusion matrices (Table~\ref{tab:aggregation}); at $K{=}5$, Bayesian aggregation neutralizes both predictors ($\eta_{\max}$ 1/12, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ 0/12), eliminating panel composition's predictive power entirely. Robustness-first selection ($\min \eta_{\max}$) achieves lowest ASR in 12/12 conditions at $K{=}3$ (Appendix~\ref{app:panel_selection}). Practitioners should match strategy to panel size: screen for individual robustness at $K{=}3$; prefer correlated robustness profiles at $K{\geq}5$. $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is computed from clean pairwise MI; recomputing MI under attacked conditions confirms stability (Spearman $\rho{>}0.91$ for 4/6 attacks), though position bias substantially disrupts MI structure ($\rho{=}0.08$ on ARC), consistent with its anomalous regression behavior (Appendix~\ref{app:attacked_keff}). Fractional logit regression \citep{papke1996econometric} yields 93.1\% directional agreement with OLS and preserves the transition pattern (Appendix~\ref{app:robustness_checks}). The random-fault contrast (\S\ref{sec:rf_contrast}) shows $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ predicts under non-adaptive noise ($R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$), with the predictability collapse under adaptive attack most pronounced at $K{=}3$ (1/12) and attenuated at $K{\geq}5$ (7/12).
```

      "content": "\\paragraph{Aggregation and Deployment.}\nAt $K{=}3$, Bayesian calibration reverses $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction (6/7 significant coefficients negative) by downweighting weak judges via calibrated confusion matrices (Table~\\ref{tab:aggregation}); at $K{=}5$, Bayesian aggregation neutralizes both predictors ($\\eta_{\\max}$ 1/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 0/12), eliminating panel composition's predictive power entirely. Robustness-first selection ($\\min \\eta_{\\max}$) achieves lowest ASR in 12/12 conditions at $K{=}3$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:panel_selection}). Practitioners should match strategy to panel size: screen for individual robustness at $K{=}3$; prefer correlated robustness profiles at $K{\\geq}5$. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is computed from clean pairwise MI; recomputing MI under attacked conditions confirms stability (Spearman $\\rho{>}0.91$ for 4/6 attacks), though position bias substantially disrupts MI structure ($\\rho{=}0.08$ on ARC), consistent with its anomalous regression behavior (Appendix~\\ref{app:attacked_keff}). Fractional logit regression \\citep{papke1996econometric} yields 93.1\\% directional agreement with OLS and preserves the transition pattern (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}). The random-fault contrast (\\S\\ref{sec:rf_contrast}) shows $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ predicts under non-adaptive noise ($R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$), with the predictability collapse under adaptive attack most pronounced at $K{=}3$ (1/12) and attenuated at $K{\\geq}5$ (7/12).",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\n\\paragraph{Regime Transition Mechanism.}\nAt $K{=}3$, a majority requires $t{=}2$; a single compromised judge flips the panel, making $\\eta_{\\max}$ the binding constraint. At $K{\\geq}5$, the adversary must compromise ${\\geq}3$ judges; high-$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ panels may present more distinct attack surfaces, consistent with the observed positive coefficient, while low-$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ panels share robust consensus. The transition threshold $K^*$ lies between 3 and 5.\n\n\\paragraph{Attack-Mechanism-Dependent Structure.}\nThe aggregate positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient masks a 6:6 directional split at $K{=}3$ (11/12 consistent at $K{=}5$; Appendix~\\ref{app:bft_reversal}). Targeted attacks are consistent with judge-specific vulnerabilities amplified by independence; shared-bias attacks are consistent with common biases diluted by diversity. Unconditional claims about diversity being ``harmful'' or ``beneficial'' are thus misleading.\n\n\\paragraph{Diversity--Capability Confound.}\nThe $r{=}0.70$ correlation between $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\eta_{\\max}$ ($r{\\approx}0.82$ in log space) reflects a structural property: maximizing diversity requires sampling across capability tiers, introducing weaker judges. VIF averages 3.0 (10/12 below 5). After controlling for $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$, $\\eta_{\\max}$ retains partial $r{=}0.31$ (unique variance ${\\approx}9.6\\%$); $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s partial $r{=}0.12$ (${\\approx}1.4\\%$). This asymmetry shows the confound does not fully explain $\\eta_{\\max}$'s advantage, but observational regression cannot reliably disentangle diversity's causal contribution from the co-occurring capability deficit (Appendix~\\ref{app:diversity_capability}). Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity}) reveals heterogeneous robustness: RV ranges from 0.005 to 0.52 across 36 conditions, with the known $\\eta_{\\max}$ confound exceeding this threshold in 28/36 conditions. Robustness increases with $K$ (RV exceeds $\\eta_{\\max}$ in 1/12 at $K{=}3$, 3/12 at $K{=}5$, 4/12 at $K{=}7$), paralleling the regime transition; this motivates the capability-matched replication below. The transition does not replicate in the API-only subset ($K{=}5$: $r(K_{\\mathrm{eff}}, \\eta_{\\max}){=}0.85$, VIF${=}4.4$), where collinearity partially inflates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ standard errors (${\\sim}2{\\times}$), attenuating its significance from 7/12 (full pool) to 3/12. However, capability-matched subsamples that break this collinearity ($r{=}0.41$) strengthen $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s emergence to 9/12, exceeding the full-pool 7/12 (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}). This pool dependence is itself informative: the transition requires sufficient capability diversity to manifest, rather than being an artifact of including weak judges. Cross-benchmark prediction further supports this interpretation: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ computed from MMLU MI predicts ARC vulnerability in 14/18 bootstrap conditions, with cross-benchmark $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ correlations of $r{\\approx}0.76$--$0.78$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_benchmark}).\n\n\\paragraph{Aggregation and Deployment.}\nAt $K{=}3$, Bayesian calibration reverses $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction (6/7 significant coefficients negative) by downweighting weak judges via calibrated confusion matrices (Table~\\ref{tab:aggregation}); at $K{=}5$, Bayesian aggregation neutralizes both predictors ($\\eta_{\\max}$ 1/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 0/12), eliminating panel composition's predictive power entirely. Robustness-first selection ($\\min \\eta_{\\max}$) achieves lowest ASR in 12/12 conditions at $K{=}3$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:panel_selection}). Practitioners should match strategy to panel size: screen for individual robustness at $K{=}3$; prefer correlated robustness profiles at $K{\\geq}5$. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is computed from clean pairwise MI; recomputing MI under attacked conditions confirms stability (Spearman $\\rho{>}0.91$ for 4/6 attacks), though position bias substantially disrupts MI structure ($\\rho{=}0.08$ on ARC), consistent with its anomalous regression behavior (Appendix~\\ref{app:attacked_keff}). Fractional logit regression \\citep{papke1996econometric} yields 93.1\\% directional agreement with OLS and preserves the transition pattern (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}). The random-fault contrast (\\S\\ref{sec:rf_contrast}) shows $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ predicts under non-adaptive noise ($R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$), with the predictability collapse under adaptive attack most pronounced at $K{=}3$ (1/12) and attenuated at $K{\\geq}5$ (7/12).\n",
